III. 
Revenue Price Out 
10 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any comments concerning the revenue price out 
11 
modifications recommended (at 19) by the SEGRP? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The Company does not agree with using a three-year average of 
13 
customers and usage by frequency for calendar years 2017-2019 in the 
14 
monthly OGIVE calculation.  The Company used one-year, calendar year 
15 
2019, in its monthly OGIVE calculation because it is more representative 
16 
of the Company’s current customer counts and usage by frequency by rate 
17 
code for each service classification as billed throughout the year.  By 
18 
implementing a three-year average in a monthly OGIVE calculation, 
19 
customer counts and usage by frequency by rate code could be over or 
20 
understated because of customer migrations within billed rate codes and 
21 


--- Page 5 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
3 of 22 
 
changes in customer usage that may occur from previous years.   Staff 
1 
provided an updated model in response to IR NMPC-39, which is attached 
2 
as part of Exhibit __ (G-RDP-1R); however, there were still instances of 
3 
negative usage by frequency for service classifications SC-1 and SC-2 in 
4 
the monthly OGIVE calculations as referenced in response to IR NMPC-
5 
38, which is attached as part of Exhibit __ (G-RDP-1R).  This creates 
6 
concern and uncertainty as to the accuracy of the SEGRP’s OGIVE 
7 
calculation.  The Company’s OGIVE calculation is more accurate and 
8 
should be used for purposes of the revenue forecast, revenue allocation, 
9 
and rate design in these proceedings because the methodology reflects a 
10 
current view of customers by rate code compared to the SEGRP’s three-
11 
year average that could understate and/or overstate the number of 
12 
customers and block usage.   
13 
  
14 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any comments concerning the SEGRP’s 
15 
recommendations (at 20-21) concerning the manner in which the 
16 
Company performs its OGIVE analysis?  
17 
A. 
Yes.  Although the Company understands the SEGRP’s rationale for 
18 
combining the SC-7 rate codes and SC-8 rate codes within its OGIVE 
19 
analysis, the Company does not agree with using a historical three-year 
20 
average of customers and usage by frequency as previously discussed.  
21 


--- Page 6 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
4 of 22 
 
Therefore, the Company recommends the use of its OGIVE calculation as 
1 
used in the Company’s revenue at current rates as shown in Exhibit___(G-
2 
RDP-2R), Schedule 1.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any comments concerning the SEGRP’s Rate 
5 
Year revenue forecast at current rates? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  The Company agrees with the SEGRP’s adjustment to the amount of 
7 
Empire Zone Rider (“EZR”) and Excelsior Jobs Program (“EJP”) 
8 
discounts that increase revenues by $26,250, along with the flow through 
9 
adjustment to late payment charges of $131 and gross revenue taxes of 
10 
$660.  In addition, Supervision and Administration revenues and 
11 
miscellaneous third-party billings have been revised to reflect the 
12 
Company’s update of general inflation, along with a revision to 
13 
Geothermal revenues to reflect the update of the pre-tax weighted average 
14 
cost of capital.  
15 
 
16 
 
As discussed previously, however, the Company does not agree with the 
17 
SEGRP’s OGIVE analysis used in the Rate Year revenue forecast at 
18 
current rates or with the sales forecast submitted by Staff Witness 
19 
McKenzie Yezzi.  The Company’s concerns with Staff Witness Yezzi’s 
20 


--- Page 7 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
5 of 22 
 
gas sales forecast are discussed in Company Witness Theodore Poe’s 
1 
rebuttal testimony. 
2 
 
In addition, there are a number of additional errors in the SEGRP’s revenue 
3 
forecast including:  (i) demand charge revenues were not calculated for 
4 
SC-12; (ii) two separate months of cumulative negative therm usage were 
5

6 
no OGIVE analysis was prepared for the first block usage for SC-5; and 
7 
(iv) no calculation of gross revenue taxes was provided on EZR/EJP 
8 
discounts.  For these reasons, the Company recommends the use of its Rate 
9 
Year revenue forecast as shown in Exhibit___(G-RDP-2R), Schedule 1.

1 
V. 
ECOS Study 
2 
Q. 
The SEGRP recommends (at 33-34) that the Company should provide 
3 
a historic ECOS study in its next base rate case.  Does the Panel agree? 
4 
A. 
The Company does not object to providing a Historic ECOSS study for 
5 
comparison purposes only in its next base rate case, provided there is no 
6 
requirement for the Company to support using the results of the study for 
7 
any purpose and does not prohibit or prejudice the Company in any way 
8 
from presenting other cost of service studies or making any revenue, cost 
9 
allocation, or rate design proposals in such case.  The Company firmly 
10 
believes that the ECOS should be prepared using the costs, rate base, and 
11 
physical units that are projected for the Rate Year, and for which the 
12 
revenue requirement, revenue allocation, and rate design will be set.  The 
13 
Company does not agree that it is reasonable to use a backward looking 
14 
historic ECOSS to establish rates. 
15 
 
16 


--- Page 9 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel

2 
on a demand basis, instead of the volumetric per therm basis the 
3 
Company used.  Does the Company accept this recommendation? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  This change would have only a very slight effect on the results of the 
5 
ECOS study because the dollar amount is small.

VI. 
Revenue Allocation 
8 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the SEGRP’s approach to revenue 
9 
allocation? 
10 
A. 
Our rebuttal addresses the gas revenue allocation submitted by the SEGRP 
11 
in response to IR NMPC-34, including attachments in response to IR 
12 
NMPC-39 referenced therein, which is being submitted as part of Exhibit 
13 
__ (G-RDP-1R).  The SEGRP’s revenue allocation is similar to the 
14 
Company’s proposed revenue allocation, with the increase assigned to each 
15 
class dependent on its position within, over, or under the band of average 
16 
return +/- 25 percent.  Although the approaches are similar, the SEGRP’s 
17 
revenue requirement calls for a decrease in overall revenue.  Therefore, the 
18 
Company recommends that the final revenue allocation be considered once 
19 
the revenue requirement is finally determined. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
8 of 22 
 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with MI Witness Pollock’s proposed revenue 
1 
allocation? 
2 
A. 
No.  While Mr. Pollock advocates moving classes closer to cost of service, 
3 
he also recommends limiting increases to the range of 0.75X-1.25X the 
4 
system average increase.  His revenue allocation proposal is similar to the 
5 
Company’s, with a slightly larger increase for SC-2 (Company proposes 5.7 
6 
percent, Pollock proposes 7.0 percent) and a slightly smaller increase for 
7 
SC-8 (Company proposes 13.9 percent, Pollock proposes 11.6 percent) and 
8 
NYSEG (Company proposes 15.7 percent, Pollock proposes 16.2 percent).  
9 
Other differences were very small.  Mr. Pollock, however, offers no reason 
10 
why the Company’s proposal is flawed or why his proposal is better.  The 
11 
Company considered issues of gradualism when evaluating revenue 
12 
allocation and rate design.  Therefore, Mr. Pollock’s revenue allocation 
13 
should be rejected. 
14 
 
15 
VII. 
Rate Design 
16 
Q. 
Please summarize the SEGRP’s rate design recommendations. 
17 
A. 
The SEGRP generally accepted the Company’s proposed rate design with 
18 
the following minor modifications: 
19 
• The SEGRP proposed somewhat different fixed monthly charges for 
20 
SC-2, SC-5 and SC-7. 
21 


--- Page 11 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
9 of 22 
 
• The Company proposed to flatten the slope (i.e., ratio of tail-block 
1 
rates to mid-block rates) for certain classes, while the SEGRP 
2 
proposed somewhat different changes to slope for SC-1, SC-2, and 
3 
SC-8. 
4 
• The SEGRP also proposed realignment of rate components for SC-
5 
10, SC-12, NYSEG, and SC-13. 
6 
The differences between the Company’s proposed rate design and the 
7 
SEGRP’s recommendations are minor.  The Company recommends final 
8 
rate design be considered once the revenue requirement is finally 
9 
determined. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please summarize Mr. Pollock’s rate design recommendations. 
12 
A. 
Mr. Pollock recommends increasing minimum charges for SC-7 and SC-8 
13 
by 50 percent.  These changes are likely to cause significantly different 
14 
intra-class bill impacts.  Although the Company recommends that final rate 
15 
design be considered once the revenue requirement is final, changes such as 
16 
Mr. Pollock proposes are unlikely to produce results that are acceptable or 
17 
reasonable. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
The SEGRP recommends (at 40-41) that the Company’s gas SC-6 
20 
Interruptible rates should be revised to provide a discount to 
21 


--- Page 12 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
10 of 22 
 
interruptible customers as further described by the SEGRP (at 50).  
1 
Does the Panel agree? 
2 
A. 
No.  The Company currently provides a discount of 20 percent from SC-8 
3 
rates, which is calculated by pricing out each group of similarly situated 
4 
customers at the total SC-8 rates including the minimum charge and all 
5 
block rates so that customers are always guaranteed at least a 20 percent 
6 
discount from the otherwise applicable firm service class rates.  
7 
Specifically, the SEGRP is recommending that the minimum charge (which 
8 
includes the first 100 therms) remain unchanged and the remaining usage 
9 
should be priced at a 40 percent discount from the SC-8 tail block rate.  The 
10 
Company believes that the current pricing methodology provides a 
11 
sufficient discount compared to the otherwise applicable firm rates.  This 
12 
proposal represents a significant decrease in SC-6 revenue from current 
13 
rates and that decrease would be borne by customers in all other classes.  In 
14 
addition, while the SEGRP indicated that their SC-6 interruptible proposal 
15 
is consistent with the current methodology used for interruptible rates at 
16 
KEDNY and KEDLI, the Company’s downstate affiliates, this is not 
17 
correct.  The currently effective interruptible rates for KEDNY and KEDLI 
18 
are set at the tail block rate of the otherwise applicable firm service 
19 
classification and do not reflect any further discount.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 13 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
11 of 22 
 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree that the IT rate cap should be reduced to 60 
1 
percent of a customer’s firm rate? 
2 
A. 
No.  As explained above, the Company believes that the current 
3 
interruptible pricing methodology provides a sufficient discount from firm 
4 
rates.

6 
VIII. Revenue Decoupling Mechanism (“RDM”) 
7 
Q. 
The SEGRP recommends (at 59) that the Company should not expand 
8 
the gas RDM to SC-5 and SC-8.  Does the Panel agree? 
9 
A. 
No.  The SEGRP recommends that the Company should not expand the 
10 
RDM to SC-5 and SC-8 because of the limited number of customers in those 
11 
service classifications.  The Company’s Rate Year gas forecast includes 140 
12 
SC-5 customers and 76 SC-8 customers for a combined total of 216 
13 
customers and represents approximately six percent of total base delivery 
14 
revenues, or approximately $24 million per year.  Because both service 
15 
classifications represent the Company’s larger Commercial and Industrial 
16 
gas customers, the Company would not be opposed to setting a combined 
17 
RDM revenue target for these classes to address Staff’s concerns regarding 
18 
the limited number of customers in each of these service classifications. 
19 
Furthermore, these service classifications are eligible to participate in 
20 
energy efficiency programs, and the RDM is intended to allow utilities to 
21 


--- Page 14 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
12 of 22 
 
promote energy efficiency to their customers without jeopardizing the 
1 
recovery of the costs incurred to serve customers. 
2 
    
3 
Q. 
MI Witness Pollock asserts (at 39) that the Company’s RDMs should 
4 
be modified so that no adjustment is triggered if the revenue variation 
5 
is solely the result of the gain or loss of customers.  Does the Panel 
6 
agree? 
7 
A. 
No.  The Company does not believe an adjustment to manually track 
8 
changes in customers for RDMs is appropriate and would be extremely 
9 
burdensome.  A revenue per class RDM target includes both fixed customer 
10 
charges and volumetric delivery charges that are dependent upon the 
11 
Company’s sales forecast when new delivery rates are approved in a rate 
12 
case proceeding.  Because both components are included in an RDM 
13 
calculation, it would not be appropriate to adjust every RDM reconciliation 
14 
class each time a customer migrates to another RDM reconciliation class or 
15 
discontinues its service.  In addition, it would be expected that new 
16 
customers would also be additions to an RDM reconciliation class, which 
17 
would offset customer losses, if any, within an RDM reconciliation class as 
18 
well.  The Company also believes that this recommendation would increase 
19 
its revenue risk and, if adopted, could require an adjustment to its cost of 
20 
capital. 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
13 of 22 
 
 
1 
In addition, as explained above, the Company is willing to set a combined 
2 
RDM target for the SC-5 and SC-8 service classifications because of the 
3 
limited number of customers to alleviate concerns regarding potential bill 
4 
impacts related to the RDM reconciliations for the customers in these 
5 
service classifications.   
6 
  
7 
IX. 
Automatic Meter Reading (“AMR”) Opt-Out Fees 
8 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the SEGRP’s recommendation (at 74-75) 
9 
concerning AMR opt-out fees? 
10 
A. 
The SEGRP made several recommendations regarding the calculation of 
11 
AMR opt-out fees.  The first recommendation is to lower the labor rate for 
12 
hourly meter readers in the meter-reading fees calculation to $32.82, as 
13 
shown in Exhibit___(SEGRP-11), Column D.  The Company agrees that 
14 
the meter reader rate should be lowered in the meter-reading fee calculation.   
15 
Based on the total annual salary and number of hourly meter reading 
16 
employees, however, the labor rate used in this calculation should be 
17 
$29.74.  The Company also lowered the labor rate for meter change-outs 
18 
from $38.97 to $38.96 to correct a rounding error.  These labor rate changes 
19 
are shown in Exhibit___(E-RDP-3R).  Second, the SEGRP suggests that the 
20 
Company conduct manual meter readings on a bi-monthly basis.  The 
21 


--- Page 16 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
14 of 22 
 
Company does not agree with this recommendation, because it could cause 
1 
a potential increase in billing exceptions (high or low bills) that require 
2 
manual review and it could also impact the accuracy of the Company’s 
3 
back-billing activities (shared metering, mixed metering, etc).  This could 
4 
also impact the customer satisfaction service quality metric as customers 
5 
are accustomed to receiving monthly, actual reads that allow them to 
6 
manage any usage fluctuations more easily.  Third, the SERGRP lowered 
7 
wrench times in Exhibit __ (SEGRP-11).  In Exhibit___(E-RDP-3R), the 
8 
Company reduced the electric meter change-out time to 17 minutes and the 
9 
combined meter change-out time to 61 minutes.  The Company does not 
10 
agree with SEGRP’s proposal of lowering the wrench time for gas meter 
11 
change-outs, as this process requires additional time to ensure a safe meter 
12 
change.  
13 
 
14 
X. 
Advanced Metering Information (“AMI”) Opt-Out Fees 
15 
Q. 
Please summarize the parties’ positions concerning AMI opt-out and 
16 
other meter replacement fees. 
17 
A. 
The SEGRP agrees (at 77) that the monthly fees developed by the Company 
18 
are based on the actual incremental costs that would be incurred to send 
19 
meter reading employees and technicians to read customer meters or install 
20 
equipment; however, the SEGRP does not support the proposed fees 
21 


--- Page 17 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
15 of 22 
 
because of policy concerns.  The SEGRP points out that these fees are 
1 
higher than the respective customer charges for gas and electric service and 
2 
further argues that fees do not always have a purely cost basis.  As the 
3 
SEGRP suggested with AMR meter reading (at 75), the SEGRP proposes 
4 
that the Company conduct the manual meter readings on a bimonthly basis.  
5 
Finally, the SEGRP lowered the wrench times for both meter reads and 
6 
meter change-outs in Exhibit___(SEGRP-11).  Ms. Panko also disagrees 
7 
with the Company’s AMI opt-out and meter replacement fees.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with either the SEGRP’s (at 78-79) or UIU 
10 
Witness Danielle Panko’s (at 78-79) recommendations concerning AMI 
11 
opt-out fees? 
12 
A. 
With respect to the SEGRP’s recommendations, the Company agrees that 
13 
the labor rate for meter readers should be lowered but disagrees with the 
14 
rate used in Exhibit ___ (SEGRP-11).  The Company disagrees with 
15 
changing meter readings to a bimonthly basis as explained above.  Lastly, 
16 
the Company disagrees with the SEGRP and UIU Witness Panko’s 
17 
recommendations that the AMI opt-out meter read and meter change fees 
18 
do not need to be based on actual costs.  The fees developed for meter 
19 
changes and meter reads should be developed on a cost basis to avoid cross 
20 
subsidies between AMI and non-AMI customers.  
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
16 of 22 
 
 
1 
XI. 
LAUF 
2 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the SEGRP’s recommendation that the 
3 
Company’s LAUF target should be set using results from the five-year 
4 
period of Gas Adjustment Clause (“GAC”) Years 2012-2016? 
5 
A. 
No.  The Company does not agree with the SEGRP’s recommendation (at 
6 
69) that the LAUF target should be revised to use the five-year period for 
7 
years 2012-2016.  The SEGRP states that the Company’s actual LAUF has 
8 
significantly fluctuated from GAC years 2015 to 2019, ranging from a 
9 
negative 0.443 percent to a positive 3.430 percent.  The Staff White Paper 
10 
recognizes that actual LAUF can vary from year to year.  For that reason, 
11 
the Commission has historically proposed the use of a five-year average to 
12 
establish the Company’s LAUF target.  Although the Company’s actual 
13 
LAUF was negative for years 2016 and 2017, both the preceding year and 
14 
subsequent year (2015 and 2018) were the highest of the five-year period 
15 
(2015-2019) used by the Company to calculate the LAUF target.  This 
16 
clearly indicates that there were likely timing issues between those years 
17 
and the actual negative LAUFs in years 2016 and 2017 were normalized 
18 
using the five-year average based on years 2015-2019.  In addition, the 
19 
SEGRP used the five-year period 2012-2016, which also includes a 
20 
negative LAUF of (0.236) percent in 2016 and a higher LAUF of 3.430 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
17 of 22 
 
percent in 2015.  For this reason, the Company disagrees with the SEGRP’s 
1 
recommendation to calculate the LAUF target using the five-year period of 
2 
2012-2016.  
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the SEGRP (at 69-70) that deliveries to 
5 
metered accounts with no associated customers (inactive accounts) 
6 
should be excluded from both metered deliveries and metered system 
7 
output in determining the LAUF target and upper/lower dead band 
8 
percentages? 
9 
A. 
The Company does not agree that inactive account usage should be 
10 
excluded from both metered deliveries and metered system output (metered 
11 
receipts) in determining the LAUF target and upper/lower dead band 
12 
percentages for the period 2012-2016 because the Company did not, and 
13 
was not required to, start tracking inactive account usage greater than 90 
14 
days until September 1, 2018.  The inactive account usage as shown in 
15 
SEGRP’s Exhibit___(SEGRP-10) includes all inactive account usage.  It 
16 
does not represent inactive account usage for greater than 90 days.  Under 
17 
Section 17.1.7 of the Company’s tariff, the Company was required as of 
18 
September 1, 2018, to remove from the LAUF calculation an estimate of 
19 
gas usage associated with accounts that have been inactive for more than 
20 
ninety days.  Therefore, the Company does not agree that it is appropriate 
21 


--- Page 20 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
18 of 22 
 
to exclude inactive account usage retroactively for any period prior to 
1 
September 1, 2018, and in fact such usage overstates the usage associated 
2 
with accounts that were inactive for greater than ninety days.  The Company 
3 
does agree to exclude inactive account usage greater than 90 days from its 
4 
calculation of the LAUF target and upper/lower dead bands in the period 
5 
for which the Company tracked the data (commencing in GAC year 2019).   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the SEGRP regarding their recommended 
8 
changes (at 70-72) to the Company’s inactive account adjustment?  
9 
A. 
The Company agrees to make a monetary adjustment using the weighted 
10 
average cost of gas multiplied by inactive account usage greater than 90 
11 
days to replace the Company’s current volumetric inactive account usage 
12 
adjustment as part of its annual GAC filings. 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with SEGRP’s recommendation (at 52-53) that 
15 
the Company’s Merchant Function Charge revenue should be updated 
16 
for Staff’s sales forecast and the proposed LAUF changes? 
17 
A. 
No.  The Company does not agree with SEGRP’s recommendation that the 
18 
Company’s Merchant Function Charge revenue should be updated for 
19 
Staff’s sales forecast and the proposed LAUF changes for reasons discussed 
20 
previously.  
21 


--- Page 21 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
19 of 22 
 
XII. 
PULP Issues 
1 
Q. 
PULP Witness Yates asserts (at 12) that the Company’s proposal to 
2 
raise its gas basic minimum service charges should be rejected.  Does 
3 
the Panel agree? 
4 
A. 
No.  The Panel understands that PULP’s term “basic service charges” refers 
5 
to the fixed monthly charges.  These charges are intended to recover the 
6 
costs of connecting and serving a customer that the Company incurs 
7 
regardless of usage.  For almost all customers, the proposed fixed charge is 
8 
less than the Company’s cost.  Therefore, the Panel does not agree that the 
9 
basic service charge should not be increased if the results of the cost of 
10 
service study support an increase.  
11 
 
12 
It appears that PULP’s rationale for rejecting increases to the fixed monthly 
13 
charges is that this would necessarily increase volumetric charges and 
14 
would therefore promote conservation.  The Company, however, has 
15 
proposed to flatten the slope (i.e., decrease the ratio of mid-block to tail 
16 
block) for most customer cases, which would also promote conservation. 
17 
 
18 


--- Page 22 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel 
 
 
 
 
20 of 22 
 
Q. 
PULP Witness Yates asserts (at 26-27) that the proposed gas rate 
1 
increases for customers enrolled in the Energy Affordability Program 
2 
(“EAP”) are greater in percentage terms than the proposed increase 
3 
for other customers.  Please explain why this is the case. 
4 
A. 
As noted by PULP, although EAP participants would see the same increase 
5 
in dollar terms, the increase is larger if stated as a percent of current bills, 
6 
because EAP participants receive a fixed dollar discount on their bills.  
7 
PULP incorrectly asserts that this is partly due to the increase in fixed 
8 
monthly charges; this is not correct unless EAP participants have different 
9 
usage profiles than non-participants, which has not been shown.    
10 
 
11 
XIII. Tariff Issues 
12

Do you agree with SEGRP’s proposed tariff language as set forth in

A. 
No.  The SEGRP did not provide testimony describing why it was 
15 
recommending the language as shown in Exhibit___(SEGRP-13) Leaf 116, 
16 
section 28.4.  The Company does not understand the need for this language, 
17 
which appears to relate to the Company’s customer migration proposal.   
18

2

3

4

5

7

8

9

10 
service classification.

13

15

16 
migrated to a different service classification will involve consideration of 
17 
more than just twelve months of historic data.  In addition, the customer’s 
18

19 
service classification will depend on the customer’s eligibility for service 
20 
under a particular service classification.  All SC-2 customers that convert to

Subject: 
Revenue Allocation 
 
 
Question: 
 
Staff’s Gas Revenue Allocation assigns decreases of 3.05% to service classes that are under-
contributing (SC8, SC12, SC13, NYSEG) and decreases of 0.61% to service classes that are 
over-contributing (SC-2, SC-10).  Please explain why Staff has determined to provide larger 
decreases to under-contributing service classes, and smaller decreases to over-contributing 
service classes.  If this calculation is in error, please provide a corrected Gas allocation. 
 
In addition, Staff multiplied the average decrease by 0.25X for under-contributing service classes 
and 1.25X for over-contributing service classes.  Please explain why these adjustments were not 
symmetrical (i.e., 0.75X and 1.25X).  If this calculation was in error, please provide a corrected 
Gas allocation, and if it is not in error, please explain. 
 
 
Response: 
 
Both of those calculations were done in error.  Under-contributing classes should get a smaller 
decrease, while over-contributing classes should get a larger decrease.  The 0.25X should also be 
changed to 0.75X.  Please see Attachment 1 to the response to IR NMPC-39 for a corrected 
version of the model (‘20-G-0381 Staff Gas Rates Model Final 12_02_2020.xlsm’), which 
includes Exhibit__(SEGRP-7) – corrected Proposed Rate Year Gas Revenue Allocation.  A 
corrected version of Exhibit___(SEGRP-7) is also provided as Attachment 2 to the response to 
IR NMPC-39.

STAFF OF THE DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC SERVICE 
RESPONSE TO INTERROGATORY/DOCUMENT REQUEST 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
Cases 20-E-0380 & 20-G-0381 
Electric & Gas Rates 
NMPC-38 continued. 
- 2 -
2. Referring to excel file ’20-G-0381 Staff Gas Rates Model Final 11_24_2020 CoB’, as
submitted in Staff’s response to NMPC-1, please explain how cumulative therms by block
can be negative as shown on excel tab ‘OGIVE Output all SC Staff’ for SC-2 Residential
Non-Heating (Nov. 2021), SC-2 Residential Heating (Sep. 2021), SC-7 Firm
Sales/Transportation (Aug. 2021) and SC-8 Firm & Standby Sales (Jun. 2022)?
3. Does Staff agree that OGIVE results using cumulative negative therms by block could skew
forecast usage by block used in a revenue price out, revenue allocation and rate design?  If
not, please explain.
Response: 
1. Linear interpolation is done in this case to fit a non-linear curve.  Negative values can occur
during the interpolation and accumulation steps when the data points, in this case therm
usage, are close together.  During the OGIVE, it is necessary to include the data points that
cover the block limits (e.g., for SC-1A - 3 therms and 50 therms) to correctly allocate the
usage into appropriate blocks.  For example, in SC-1A Residential Non Heat August-21, the
step 3.25 is very close to the 3 therm step required because Block 1 for that Service Class
ends after 3 therms.
2. In reference to SC-2, see response to Question 1.  Regarding SC-7 and SC-8, an error was
found in the formula to calculate the three-year average historic bill frequency data used in
the ogive analysis.  A corrected version of the model, which contains a corrected OGIVE
analysis, is included in Staff’s Response to IR NMPC-39 as Attachment 1.  This correction to
the model removes all negative therm values associated with SC-7 and SC-8.
3. Yes, negative usage could skew forecast usage by block used in a revenue price out, revenue
allocation and rate design; however, for SC-1A and SC-2 the effects of the negative values
are de minimus.
Exhibit___(G-RDP-1R)
Rebuttal

numerous SC-7 seasonal customers?

submitted in Staff’s response to NMPC-1, does Staff agree that its SC-7 Small
Firm/Transportation service classification proposed Rate Year rate design for Blocks 2-4
does not use the correct Block usage as shown on excel tab ‘OGIVE Output all SC Staff’?  If

STAFF OF THE DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC SERVICE 
RESPONSE TO INTERROGATORY/DOCUMENT REQUEST 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
Cases 20-E-0380 & 20-G-0381 
Electric & Gas Rates 
NMPC-39 continued. 
- 2 -
6. Referring to Staff’s calculation as described in question 5 above, does Staff agree that its
calculation for SC-5 Block 1 usage would not be deemed appropriate for the Company’s SC-
5 customers who have seasonal usage?
7. Please explain why Staff did not calculate Merchant Function Charge revenue at current rates
for SC-2 Transportation, SC-5 Firm Transportation, SC-7 Firm Transportation and SC-12
Distribution Generation Transportation.  If the revenues were erroneously omitted, please
provide an updated file with the correction.
8. Referring to excel file ’20-G-0381 Staff Gas Rates Model Final 11_24_2020 CoB’, as
submitted in Staff’s response to NMPC-1, does Staff agree that its Gross Receipts Tax
(“GRT”) calculation for SC-1 Residential service classification should have used the
commodity GRT tax rate on the Merchant Function Charge revenues as opposed to the
delivery GRT tax rate?  If yes, please provide an updated file with the correction.
Response: 
1. For service classes that only have two blocks and low block limits of the number of therms,
the usage in that first block is calculated as the maximum therm usage of that block
multiplied by the customer count because that is the maximum revenue that could be
collected from the first block.
2. Due to the higher therm limit of SC-7 block 1, which is 2100 therms, the Panel performed an
ogive analysis on SC-7.  However, the Panel did not link the block 1 usage to the SC-7 ogive
analysis.  Instead, the Panel erroneously used the methodology listed in response to Question
1. We have corrected this in the model provided as Attachment 1 to this response.
3. Attachment 1 to this response contains a corrected version of the Excel model - ‘20-G-0381
Staff Gas Rates Model Final 12_03_2020.xlsm.’  This now correctly aligns the values from
the tab ‘OGIVE Output All Staff’ with the correct blocks in the tab ‘Rate Design Staff.’  In
addition, Attachment 2 to this response contains a corrected Exhibit__(SEGRP-7) – Proposed
Rate Year Gas Revenue Allocation, Attachment 3 to this response contains a corrected
Exhibit__(SEGRP-8) – Summary of Proposed Gas Rate Year Increases, and Attachment 4 to
this response contains a corrected Exhibit__(SEGRP-9) – Detailed Gas Bill Impacts for Rate
Year.
4. Yes, the formulas have been corrected in the ‘Rate Design Staff’ tab and the correct months
have been brought into the summer and winter rates.  This is corrected in the model provided
as Attachment 1 to this response.

STAFF OF THE DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC SERVICE 
RESPONSE TO INTERROGATORY/DOCUMENT REQUEST 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
Cases 20-E-0380 & 20-G-0381 
Electric & Gas Rates 
Request No.: 
NMPC-54 
Requested By: 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a 
National Grid 
Information Requested of: 
Staff Electric and Gas Rates Panel 
Date of Request: 
December 4, 2020 
Response Due Date: 
December 15, 2020 
Date of Response: 
December 10, 2020 
Name of Respondent: 
Jennifer Park, Sandra Hart, and Brian Grode 
Subject: 
LAUF 
Question: 
Staff Electric and Gas Rates Panel states on pages 69-70 of their direct testimony “We excluded 
deliveries to metered accounts with no associated customer (inactive accounts) from both 
metered deliveries and metered system output.  In other words, we calculated the LAUF target 
and dead-band excluding gas metered to inactive accounts, as shown in Exhibit__(SEGRP-10), 
Schedule 3”.  Staff Electric and Gas Rates Panel states on page 72 of their direct testimony “In 
lieu of making a volumetric adjustment to the LAUF incentive, we recommend the Company 
reduce its revenues in the GAC reconciliation by the inactive accounts adjustment, which should 
be calculated by multiplying the volumes associated with inactive accounts by the weighted 
average cost of gas.” 
Please clarify Staff’s LAUF proposal in full and please provide updated exhibits if deemed 
necessary. 
Response: 
Attachment 1 shows Staff Electric and Gas Rates Panel’s updated LAUF model.  First, the Panel 
would like to clarify that on page 70, line 3, “metered system output” should be read as “metered 
receipts.”  Next, we inadvertently did not remove the inactive accounts from the metered 
receipts, as shown in Exhibit__(SEGRP-10), Schedule 3.  Attachment 2 shows the Corrected 
Exhibit__(SEGRP-10), Schedule 3, which includes our removal of inactive accounts from the 
metered receipts, removal of the SC-4 line which has no associated gas usage, revision of the 
heading from “Cases 20-G-0380 & 20-G-0381” to “Cases 20-E-0380 & 20-G-0381”, and 
revision of the line numbers (i.e., Net Metered Deliveries line has changed from Line 17 to Line 
15).  As a result of these changes, the Panel’s proposed LAUF target decreased from 1.667% to 
1.519%; the upper dead band limit decreased from 2.667% to 2.519%; and the lower dead band 
limit decreased from 0.667% to 0.519%.  Attachment 3 to this response shows the Corrected 
Exhibit__(SEGRP-10), Schedule 4 which reflects our changes to our recommended LAUF target 
Exhibit___(G-RDP-1R)
Rebuttal
Page 38 of 44


--- Page 65 ---
STAFF OF THE DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC SERVICE 
RESPONSE TO INTERROGATORY/DOCUMENT REQUEST 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
Cases 20-E-0380 & 20-G-0381 
Electric & Gas Rates 
NMPC-54 continued. 
- 2 -
and associated dead bands.  Finally, Attachment 4 to this response shows the Corrected 
Exhibit_(SEGRP-10), Schedule 5, which reflects the heading change from “Niagara Mohawk 
Gas Corporation d/b/a National Grid” to “National Grid Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid). 
As stated on page 72 of the Panel’s direct testimony, “In lieu of making a volumetric adjustment 
to the LAUF incentive, we recommend the Company reduce its revenues in the GAC 
reconciliation by the inactive accounts adjustment, which should be calculated by multiplying 
the volumes associated with inactive accounts by the weighted average cost of gas.”  The Panel 
recommends that any volumes associated with the inactive accounts be excluded in the actual 
LAUF calculation.  In addition, the Panel also recommends that the Company reduce its GAC 
revenues by the amount associated with the inactive accounts using the calculation depicted in 
Attachment 4.  The calculation arrives at the revenue associated with inactive accounts by 
multiplying the volumes associated with inactive accounts by the weighted average cost of gas, 
therefore reducing the average weighted cost of gas revenues.

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION d/b/a NATIONAL GRID 
Case Nos. 20-E-0380 and 20-G-0381 
Electric and Gas Rates 
Request for Information 
FROM: 
Multiple Intervenors, Michael B. Mager 
TO: 
National Grid, Gas Rate Design Panel 
SUBJECT:   Gas Rate Design
Request: 
59.
In the direct testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel (see, e.g., pages 52-53), the
Company projects that customers would migrate from and to certain gas service
classifications; to wit: S.C. 2, S.C. 3, S.C. 5, S.C. 7, and S.C. 8. It appears that certain
migrations relate to the Company’s proposal to eliminate S.C. 3. That noted, explain why
the Company is projecting existing S.C. 5 customers to migrate to S.C. 7 and S.C. 8.
What is the basis for such migrations? Are such migrations voluntary (i.e., customer
elections) or mandatory (i.e., imposed by the Company)? If the eligibility criteria with
respect to consumption is not changing for S.C. 5, why would S.C. 5 customers migrate
to S.C. 7 or S.C. 8? Explain the response in full.
Response:  
During development of the proposal to revise the Company’s gas tariff (P.S.C. No. 219) as 
described in the direct testimony of the Gas Rate Design Panel (pages 51-53), the Company 
reviewed all current customers on S.C. 2, 3, 5, 7, and 8 and their annual usage billed during fiscal 
year 2019.  Based on this preliminary review, the Company determined that some customers may 
be more appropriately placed under different service classifications based on their FY 2019 
usage profile.  Once the proposal is approved, the Company plans to conduct a more 
comprehensive review of each customer to confirm the preliminary analysis, using the criteria 
described below, before migrating any customer from their current service classification.    
The Company will review and evaluate each customer’s usage for the most recently available 12-
month period, along with historical usage patterns, giving consideration to any potential impact 
on customer usage from the COVID-19 pandemic.  If a customer meets the usage eligibility 
requirements based on the twelve-month period alone, the customer would be qualified to remain 
on their existing rate.  Those customers identified as not meeting the usage requirements would 
be assigned to the appropriate service classification if exceptions were not noted during the 
review period (i.e., COVID-19 pandemic-related closures/change in usage, new equipment 
installations, major equipment repairs, data/metering issues, data/plans to demonstrate achieving 
Exhibit___(G-RDP-1R)
Rebuttal
Page 43 of 44


--- Page 70 ---
Form 103 
projected volumes, etc.).  Given the Company’s proposal to achieve the same delivery rates for 
similarly situated customers whether they are sales service or transportation customers, the 
Company deemed it appropriate to review commercial and industrial service classifications prior 
to the implementation of new delivery rates.  In addition, the Company will develop a 
communication plan for the affected customers and implement an annual review process to 
ensure customers are on the appropriate service classification. 
To ensure customers are being billed under the proper service classification and paying the 
appropriate delivery rates in its gas tariff, the customer migrations would be imposed by the 
Company if the customer did not meet service classification usage eligibility requirements or 
qualify for any exceptions discussed previously.    
Name of Respondent: 
Date of Reply:  
Melissa M. Barnes 
October 26, 2020
